ParlaMint-DK_20160513090256	The debate is open. Minister of Finance, please.
ParlaMint-DK_20160513090310	The Socialists have put forward a proposal requiring the government to return the priority contribution to the municipalities. I am surprised when the government has already said that all funds from the re-prioritisation contribution in the context of the economic negotiations and the negotiations on the Finance Act will be returned to the municipalities for 2017 to priority areas. The Socialists probably want us to say that the re-prioritisation contribution must be returned to the municipalities for all the years to come, so that we are already laying down the financial framework for 2018 and beyond. But we can't. This is not how we organise economic policy. We will make the priorities for 2018 and forward as usual in the economic negotiations and financial negotiations in those years, because we have a principle that the economic priorities are made for one year at a time, because the Finance Act is 1 year, and the agreements on the local and regional economies are 1 year. This was the case under the previous government, and it is, of course, the case now, because we need to be able to prioritize from year to year if there are unforeseen additional costs and because we need to be able to control expenditure and react when the key conditions of the economy change. The need to manage expenditure right now is quite obvious. We can see that the public economy is being challenged in a number of areas. We are in an economic situation where falling oil prices give the state less revenue from oil and gas extraction in the North Sea than expected. In September of last year, when we decided on the room for manoeuvre for fiscal policy towards 2020, it amounted to about 15 billion. kr. Since then, structural revenue from the North Sea has been downgraded by 5 billion. kr. That is why we have now had to reduce the economic scope for the next four years to approx. 10 billion kr. Overall, the structural revenues from the North Sea since 2013, i.e. in just 3 years, have fallen by just under 20 billion. kr. This represents almost half of the annual expenditure on primary school. Thus, the situation is quite different from that of the previous government. It would therefore also be irresponsible, in this light, to follow the Socialists' proposal to establish the municipal framework for 2018 and beyond now. In addition to a reduced economic space, the influx of asylum puts considerable pressure on public finances. For 2016 alone, 10.9 billion has been budgeted. kr. to the area of asylum and integration. If the influx remains at a high level in the coming years, the pressure on expenditure will increase by 2020. This increases the demands for strict priorities in expenditure policy in the future. That is why the whole public sector needs to contribute to the freeing up of resources so that we can give priority to citizens' welfare, so that we can spend the money where they do the most good for the Danes. The whole of the public sector must be part of a strict priority for resources, and all parts of the public sector must contribute to the smarter use of resources. For this reason, the government has considered it necessary and appropriate to extend the re-priority contribution to the municipalities. In the state we have had a re-priority contribution for many years. We have now spread this to all major state operating areas, including education and culture. Thus, a long-standing principle in force in the state has been extended to the municipalities. The re-prioritisation contribution is, in fact, a tool that supports the efficient use of resources. At the same time, it strengthens the ability to prioritise across sectors, so that money is spent where it is most beneficial. This means that we will have the opportunity to prioritise important areas such as health, the elderly and security. The government wants a strong public sector. In 2016, 1.9 billion. kr. of the re-prioritised contribution in 2016 returned to the municipalities for priority actions in the bourgeois service areas. Here comes the promise of 1 billion. kr. to a dignified care for the elderly with the 2016 Finance Act. Overall, therefore, the services of the municipalities have been increased by 0.5 billion. kr. From 2015 to 2016. The municipalities thus have more money for service in 2016, than they had in 2015. In addition, developments are under the recent Social Democratic-led governments, where the municipal service framework was reduced by several laps. From 2012 to 2015, municipalities could spend 3 billion. kr. less on service. That is why it is also strange that the Socialists are creating a picture of the government's desire for massive cuts in the public sector. On the contrary, with the re-priority contribution, the government wants to create the conditions for us to give priority to core welfare. The Prime Minister has also stated that the money from the re-priority contribution cannot go to tax cuts. The government therefore wants the money from the re-priority contribution to be spent on citizens' services and for the money to be spent where the need is greatest in the year in question. The re-priority contribution shall apply to the municipalities under one. It will therefore be up to each municipality to determine how the re-priority contribution is to be implemented locally. Of course, we should not sit here at Christiansborg and decide how the municipalities realise the necessary efficiency improvements. After all, some municipalities are expected to have better opportunities to adjust service costs than others and thus also better opportunities to realise a larger share of a re-priority contribution. It is based on dialogue between municipalities and local political priorities in each municipality. In this way, it is ensured that each municipality can organise the overall effort on the basis of what creates the greatest value locally. I think it is realistic that the municipalities can streamline by 1%. Every year? Yeah, I mean that. I do not buy the premise that there can be no more efficiency in the municipalities without harming the core welfare. I fully recognise that the municipalities are in the process of improving efficiency. The municipalities themselves also report that since 2007 they have gradually increased the efficiency of their work, so that in many municipalities the efficiency of their work is increased by 2%. of expenditure per year. The municipalities themselves have previously indicated that since 2011 they have increased the efficiency of between 2.4 and 3 billion. kr. a year. They have among other things: more efficient for about 1 billion. kr. alone by coordinating purchases and about 1 billion. kr. by digitisation in recent years. I cannot therefore understand the view that, if we are to streamline and achieve lower costs in the municipalities, it is by dismissing people. I am, of course, impressed as a finance minister by the efforts made by the municipalities all over the country to improve efficiency. I am quite sure that we can find many examples in the municipal landscape in municipalities that find new ways of improving efficiency. It is precisely the effort made by the municipalities that says that the municipal hinterland at least believes that we can create a better and more efficient public sector in the future. Then you ask me whether a re-priority contribution automatically leads to poorer care for the elderly and primary schools, then the answer is clear: no, I do not think so. The municipalities have a service framework of just under 240 billion. kr., and it should be possible to spend some of the money better than we do today, among other things. by learning from each other, through digitisation, better procurement, by putting more tasks into competition, etc. All parts of the public sector, state, regions and municipalities must continue to be considerably more efficient, because at a time of tight economic conditions, we must continue to get more out of the taxpayers' crowns. This is a prerequisite for us also to be able to afford a strong welfare society in the future. We cannot live with a public sector cast in concrete. In the motion for a resolution, the Socialists are calling for the government to call for broad negotiations on the reversal of the re-prioritisation contribution. Frankly, I do not understand what the Socialists are calling for. The precise implementation of the funds will, of course, take place in the economic negotiations with the municipalities this summer and in the financial negotiations in the autumn. So, if the parties in the red bloc want to influence the distribution of money, they will have to participate constructively in the financial legislation negotiations in the autumn, and I would very much like to encourage them to do so. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160513104150	Thank you. There are no more brief comments. The next rapporteur is Mrs Josephine Fock, the alternative.
ParlaMint-DK_20160513104216	Thank you for the floor. With this proposal, the municipalities will be given a guarantee that the money raised by the re-priority contribution will be returned to the municipalities. At the same time, this means that the municipalities, which are currently under financial pressure, have a guarantee of how much money they actually have in everything, and we support that in the alternative. At the same time, we are quite generally opposed to the re-prioritisation contribution, because we believe that the re-prioritisation contribution gives the municipalities an unfair uncertainty which makes it difficult for the municipalities to budget in the longer term too. And it is important, in order to be able to implement a municipal policy, to know what you have to have at your disposal. We believe that, from the tax freeze to the expenditure ceiling, the possibility for municipalities to decide their own budgets has been reduced, and we believe in the alternative that municipalities should be freed. That is why we want to go further than what the Socialists are proposing here. We all want to cancel the re-prioritisation contribution and also hear it, and we think it is very nice that the Socialists will table an amendment to their own motion for a resolution in which they will withdraw the re-prioritisation contribution. The re-prioritisation contribution is the latest of a number of measures which have reduced municipal autonomy and the municipalities' right to prioritise. There are several requirements for standards and streamlining in the different areas of action such as the elderly, employment and integration. There is a tax freeze and real minus growth, which has meant that every year the municipalities dismantle sound and sensible measures due to huge savings, and there is a budgetary ceiling that limits the possibility of transferring money from year to year and thus making investments for the future. And the latest on the tribe here is the re-priority contribution. We believe it is important that the municipalities know what they have to budget with and what they can tell their citizens if we are to take self-government seriously. In this way, we believe that the re-priority contribution is a flawed construction. The municipalities are told to save 2.4 billion. kr., and that they may get them back, but maybe also earmarked for one thing or another. We do not think this is a serious way of budgeting. In addition to making it more difficult for municipalities to budget, the approach is also an expression of a lack of confidence and humility in the abilities of the municipalities, when you think you are centrally better at caring for the citizens locally. We believe that the municipalities should be trusted, and we should not think that they know better than they do locally. The municipalities want the best for their citizens, and the solutions are usually better, the closer to the citizens the reality exists. We lack greater respect for local self-government and we lack confidence that the municipalities can give priority on behalf of their citizens rather than act as agents of the policy of the current government at all times. However, although the proposal does not go as far as we would have liked, it goes a bit, and we look forward to seeing the amendment from the Socialists where they will completely abolish the re-priority contribution. That proposal will be supported by the alternative.

ParlaMint-DK_20160513104533	Thank you to the Social Democrats for bringing up again this crucial debate on the Danish economy and the re-priority contribution. Hopefully, our position on the re-priority contribution is well known. We're against it. We think this is the wrong way to do it, and we think it is primarily because the methodology chosen by the government is very centralistic. After all, we all know that there is still room for improvement in various areas of the public sector, because new technologies and new methods are emerging and priorities can be set differently. However, the unconscious green harvesting method chosen by the government is very unsympathetic and can hardly fail to go beyond the well-being and core services of the municipalities in a situation where the increasing number of refugees is already under heavy pressure. In addition, it is almost where we think the chain jumps most of the time that one has chosen a method which means that one lets the municipalities make all the difficult choices, do the hard work, reap the method, and then you take the money into Christiansborg, where the government then sits with the Danish People's Party and others a late night hour and distribute the money to the areas where you think you can get the most voters for them. We do not think this is a very sympathetic method, nor do we think it is a very strategic method, which is why we are opposed to it. ParlaMint-DK_20160513105450	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mr Seligman. Jonas Dahl, Socialist People's Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20160513105455	Thank you. Thank you, too, for today's debate. I think it illustrates very well the challenges we face and, above all, the challenges that the Minister of Finance faces in these weeks in connection with the negotiations that are taking place under the auspices of the National Association of Municipalities these days. That is why the Socialists have tabled this motion for a resolution. So on the content side: I am in fact one hundred per cent in agreement with what has been said by the Socialists. Because what is the problem here is not, as several rapporteurs have said, whether things can be done wiser and better and more effectively in the public sector. Yes, of course you can. I think you do that everywhere every day, and I think that is a task for all of us. The question is, do you have to move money out? And this calculation, which you could say is just a small re-priority contribution, costs the municipalities up to 7 billion. kr. Towards 2019. It is money that I doubt can be made more efficient by itself. And if you can finally make that amount more effective, and we can then take a long philosophical discussion on that; I guess that Mr. Ole Birk Olesen would like to take part in it in a few moments. It is basically about whether we should develop welfare or whether we should just look at it being cut. I think it is basically what we do in the SF that it can and must be done better, but I also believe that we have to look at the fact that there are some places in the public sector where things need to be done better. The demography itself suggests that more funds will be needed to ensure the proper welfare of the elderly, and we have challenges with the norms of our day institutions. I also believe that few people have passed by the fact that despite the reform of primary school, which in many ways helps to raise primary school to a higher level, challenges remain. The question then is: can you do things better and constantly focus on the need for fewer funds? Or should we say that we can at least expect to have something similar, and then the starting point can still be to do better? I am, in fact, in favour of the last solution, because I believe basically that this is both about working on how to achieve greater efficiency, but that it is also about ensuring that municipalities have a framework that they can actually count on and trust. And that is perhaps what, as far as I am concerned, will be the fundamental problem of the re-prioritisation contribution. I would like to acknowledge that a government and a majority of parliaments can always go to the municipalities and say: you must save a given amount; that is our political view; it is we who set the framework for the municipalities, and we choose that this year you should spend a little less or a little more. That is the opportunity and right of a political majority. The problem is here when you go in and park that discussion in a re-priority contribution. Because there is no question of the municipalities having a guarantee of what they have. But it is also not about the municipality's National Association being able to enter into a discussion this summer and say: well, when we have concluded the negotiations, we also have an idea of what we have available in the coming years. No, the situation you will find yourself in in the municipalities, if the re-priority contribution is implemented here, is that a number of funds are being taken from the municipalities, which will then have to make budget agreements during the autumn, knowing that there is a large X-factor, so that they may, if they are lucky, get something afterwards in connection with the Finance Act. It may also be that they are unfortunate and that the money has been spent on some tax cuts or some cheaper cars. It is, of course, an absurd situation to put the municipalities in if, at the same time, we demand that there should be long-term objectives. And that is, in fact, some of what has been done, including with the budget law, which we, the SF, have supported. It is precisely about providing a long-term framework to the municipalities so that they can make a planning that actually ensures that the municipalities know what they have to do with today and tomorrow, but also several years into the future, so that they can plan some activities and actually look at what improvements that may cost a little now, but actually give a boost. This can be both in relation to preventive action, but it can also be a development effort for children, for the elderly, in primary school and in a large number of other areas. That possibility will be far, far more difficult if you say: Now we take some money, and then you can be left with an uncertainty that you might get some funds available later. Perhaps that's where I'm most surprised that the Danish Folkepartie has jumped on this track. Because I actually understood it that way, and I have great respect for that, Mr President. Kristian Thulesen Dahl and the Danish People's Party would like to discuss how we improve our welfare. That's a good signal, let's try to do something from those frames. And now with the Social Democrats and Mr. Benny Engelbrecht says that they are in fact willing to drop the entire re-priority contribution with the amendment that has been proposed here today, so it also underlines the possibility of creating a welfare policy majority, which actually recognises that there are challenges in the municipalities, recognises that there is a need for a welfare boost, but also recognises a need for long-term planning and a need for the possibility of long-term planning in the municipalities. And that is unfortunately what they will not be able to do if we vote down this proposal, which unfortunately the majority of citizens are proposing. Against this background, I must inform you that the SF naturally supports the motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160513113026	Thank you to the rapporteur for the questions. Then I give the floor to the Minister for Finance to answer the question.
ParlaMint-DK_20160513113039	The Enhedslisten has asked me to explain the economic situation in the municipalities, which we have already discussed earlier today in connection with motion for a resolution B 89. In this context, it is of course relevant to look at the overall public economy and the room for manoeuvre that we have in the public economy in the coming years. As I also made clear in my speech in this Chamber earlier today, in September 2015, the last time we expressed the room for manoeuvre in fiscal policy was over 15 billion. kr. Towards 2020. Since then, structural revenue from the North Sea has been downgraded by 5 billion. kr., and that is why we have now had to reduce the economic scope over the next 4 years to approx. 10 billion kr. Overall, this means that we have less money to do well in the public economy than we previously thought. From a historical point of view, we have limited room for manoeuvre. Overall, the structural revenues from the North Sea since 2013, i.e. in just 3 years, have fallen by just under 20 billion. kr. This represents almost half of the annual expenditure on primary school, and thus the situation is quite different from that of the previous government. At the same time, the influx of refugees and migrants means that the pressure of expenditure within the scope will increase in the coming years. This increases the need for sharp priorities, just as there is a need to look at whether a number of public tasks cannot be solved smarter so that you get more money. In other words, we have a common challenge in developing our welfare societies at a time when strict financial frameworks are a basic condition. There is therefore a need for us to ensure together sustained development in the public sector and to ensure that the money is given priority where it is most beneficial to the Danes. This is among other things. In view of the prospect of a tight framework, the government has found it necessary and appropriate to extend the re-priority contribution to the municipalities. The re-prioritisation contribution is a tool that has been used in the state for many years. It strengthens the ability to repriot across sectors and supports ongoing efficiency and development. And yes, it is also a tool that makes great demands on efficiency and priorities in each municipality and in each state organisation. However, this is a necessary tool for ensuring the sustainable development of the public sector, not least in view of the tight framework for the public economy. We have to recognise that there are no easy alternatives if we are to be able to afford a strong welfare state in future too. The opposition should also be the first to recognise that, as you know, it was under the former government, in other words with the Enhedslisten as a parliamentary support party, that the municipalities could spend 3 billion. kr. less on service. That reduction in the framework I suppose the previous government considered necessary in the light of the limited room for manoeuvre in the overall public economy, in the same way as now when we say that it is necessary for municipalities to continue to make their economies more effective. That is why one might well wonder when the red block now, when they do not have government power, paints a picture that the lower framework automatically gives worse public service. So that we have now reached the point where things cannot be made smarter tomorrow than they are done today and that we should not be able to re-prioritize across the public sector. I don't agree with that picture at all. Firstly, I do not believe that we can live with a public sector that is cast in concrete and where we cannot re-prioritize to those areas where the need turns out to be greatest. Secondly, I do not buy the premise that the municipalities with a total service framework of just under 240 billion. kr. It is not possible to continue to achieve great efficiency gains. On the contrary, I am convinced that the municipalities can continue to make things smarter by learning from each other, through digitalisation, by placing more tasks on the competition level in the same way as the municipalities have done in recent years, where according to KL each year the municipalities have made it more efficient for between 2.5 and 3.0 billion. kr. There is therefore a need for the municipalities to do this in the future too. The funds we are releasing from the re-prioritised contribution will be continuously translated over the government period into priority actions in the citizen-centred welfare where we consider the need to be greatest. 1.9 billion kr. In 2016, the re-prioritised contribution was returned to the municipalities for priority actions in the bourgeois service areas. And with the 2016 Finance Act, we chose to give priority to the elderly area when we set aside 1 billion. kr. to a dignified care for the elderly. Overall, the services of the municipalities were thus increased by 0.5 billion. kr. From 2015 to 2016. This means that the municipalities actually have more money available for service in 2016 than they had in 2015. Thus, this government has strengthened municipal welfare since its accession. For 2017, we have decided to prioritize the municipal welfare areas, among others. in order to ensure the citizen-friendly welfare in the light of the influx of refugees, by transferring all funds from the re-priority contribution to the municipalities in the context of the economic negotiations and the Finance Act 2017. In this context, it is also important to note that the government and KL prior to Easter concluded an agreement on the integration area, in which the municipalities, among other things, are: was given an economic help to establish new homes. At the same time, we have agreed on a large number of regulatory simplifications which make integration efforts in the municipalities better and cheaper, thereby supporting the use of resources in the municipalities. Earlier today, the Social Democrats have proposed that we guarantee that the municipalities will also receive the re-priority contribution back in 2018 and in subsequent years. To give that guarantee I think is irresponsible, not least in view of the current economic situation. Because, as I have also made clear in the past, it is our responsibility as a responsible government to give priority to annual funding for those areas where the need is greatest and where, for example, there is one. There are unforeseen additional costs, just as we must be able to control expenditure and react when the key conditions of the economy change, for example. when structural revenues from the North Sea since 2013 have decreased by just under 20 billion. kr. Therefore, we will not already take a position on the application of the 2018 re-prioritisation contribution. Having said that, we have made it clear that the re-prioritisation contribution will go to citizen-friendly services, but whether it becomes health, the elderly or something quite third, we will take a position on in the relevant economic negotiations and finance laws in accordance with normal practice. Another important part of the municipal economy concerns municipal investment. Public investment has been historically high in recent years. This also applies to municipal investment. The high level of investment has supported growth and employment during the economic crisis. As economic growth grows, there is a need for a normalisation of the level of public investment, and it is in line with a sensible stabilisation policy that easing during a recession is matched by corresponding austerity when the business cycle improves. In the coming years, therefore, public investment will have to normalise at the level of the level financed. If that does not happen, we will have to use our limited scope to finance investment levels, and thus there will be even less priority for service in the coming years. In the case of municipalities, the first steps to adapt have already been taken, among other things. also under the previous government. The next steps will be taken in the coming years to ensure that public investment corresponds to the levels financed. Overall, the financial framework of the municipalities will also be tight in the coming years. This is necessary at a time when the framework for the public economy is narrow. The tight framework will require the municipalities to make the necessary priorities and efficiency, in the same way as it will require the regions and the state. It will not be an easy task, I fully acknowledge that. But does this mean that it is not possible and that it will therefore automatically lead to worse service? No, it certainly does not. Because there are still plenty of opportunities to make municipalities more efficient by learning from each other, by digitalising, by putting more tasks into competition, etc. We must also do so if we are to ensure that we also have a strong welfare society in the future without leaving the bill for future generations. Having said that, however, it is also worth remembering that last year this government chose to prioritise more funds for the municipalities than the year before and that for 2017 it has been decided that the municipalities will have the same funds available for service as they have in 2016. Thank you for the floor.

The Danish Parliament therefore requires the government to cancel the 2017 priority contribution before the conclusion of the next municipal agreement. In view, among other things, of the increasing number of older people and the municipalities' tasks in receiving and integrating people fleeing war, the Danish Parliament is instructing the government in the forthcoming negotiations with the KL to work for an economic solution in 2017, which does not include a re-priority contribution, which requires municipalities to cut welfare and which means that municipalities do not have economic space for investment in people to ensure a sustainable economy in the long term. • V 59).

And the municipalities do not have that right now, although they want to do the job and have high ambitions. It requires a room for action, both economic and bureaucratic, to invest in people if it is to be done long-term. This means that the municipalities know what they have to budget with. The re-prioritisation contribution is the latest of a number of measures that have reduced municipal autonomy and the municipalities' right to prioritize. I believe that there is no confidence in the municipalities, that is, once they receive money and then have them taken away again, so that the government can give priority to them as if they do not know what is best for their citizens. We think that construction is completely wrong. In the eyes of the alternative, we must have less state and more society, and this means that solutions must be found close to the citizens in the municipalities, where we know best. That is why we also hope that the government will take the objections of the opposition and the municipalities seriously and abolish the re-priority contribution. We hope that the municipalities will have an economic space to invest in people to ensure economic and social sustainability in the long term.

ParlaMint-DK_20160513132744	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for Industry and Growth.
ParlaMint-DK_20160513132754	Thank you. The alternative has proposed a parliamentary decision to introduce a 2-year entrepreneurial benefit. The alternative has also proposed a parliamentary resolution establishing an international entrepreneurship programme with two associated international entrepreneurship houses. It is, of course, the case that the two proposals are now being dealt with together, and that is why, in my reply, I would also like to take both of them with me and make a statement about the government's position on both of them. With regard to the proposal for a 2-year entrepreneurial benefit, the government is invited to introduce a benefit equal to 80%. of a normal daily allowance rate with associated advice, sparring and mentoring. It is also proposed that the expected 20 per cent savings should be made. of the normal daily allowance rate must be used to co-finance advice, sparring and mentoring. In the past, there has been a similar system of entrepreneurship benefits. The scheme was closed in 1998 because it did not work as intended. Entrepreneurship simply did not help create healthy and sustainable businesses. The performance gave a particular competitive advantage to the entrepreneurs who started up businesses with a service compared to other new and, for that matter, also existing companies. Moreover, with regard to entrepreneurs, many fell back into unemployment after the benefit ceased, and some actually also received a substantial VAT and tax debt. As a result, the former entrepreneurial service actually served as an unhealthy state aid that artificially kept non-competitive companies alive. Experience from the 1990s points to the need to be very careful in setting up general schemes to provide public support for long-term unemployed people wishing to start up their own businesses. That is why the government cannot support the proposal put forward by the alternative. The motion for a resolution on the creation of an international entrepreneurship programme calls on the government to establish in concrete terms two international entrepreneurship houses with space for 200 entrepreneurs each and associated advice and sparring from entrepreneurship experts. It is proposed that 100 international work and travel grants should be distributed annually to particularly promising young Danish entrepreneurial talents. In addition, it is proposed that 100 work and residence permits be issued annually to particularly promising international entrepreneurs, who will also receive an initial capital of DKK 250,000. Each. Overall, I believe that the intentions behind the proposal can be sensible in many ways. But we also have to bear in mind that there is no provision, I think, for any kind of funding of the proposal. After all, this government attaches the utmost importance to the link between the expenditure we have and the revenue that comes in. At the same time, the proposals from the Alternative have considerably overlapped with existing private and public initiatives. Private entrepreneurial environments in Denmark are booming, and this is very welcome. We see in these years that many different private actors offer suitable office communities to both Danish and international entrepreneurs, in some places also with different opportunities for sparring associated. Therefore, it is difficult to see the need for the state to create international entrepreneurship houses, which will, of course, compete with private offers. In relation to travel grants to Danish entrepreneurs, it is true that we need internationally oriented entrepreneurs who think globally from the beginning. However, we already have various programmes to help Danish entrepreneurs into the world today. Recently, I myself was involved in launching the Danish Bridge network, which among other things. to help Danish entrepreneurs access networks in Silicon Valley, where they can hopefully be able to attract new knowledge and for that matter also capital. In addition, there is a programme such as Erasmus for young entrepreneurs, where, as a Danish entrepreneur, you can come out and learn from another European entrepreneur. It is therefore possible for the most ambitious entrepreneurs to acquire the necessary knowledge and experience to start a business. In addition, the distribution of 100 work and travel grants per year will also lead, I would say, to a significant bureaucratic model without guaranteeing any significant positive economic impact. If we look at the attraction of foreign entrepreneurs, in many ways the idea is also exciting, but there is already a pilot scheme called Start-up Denmark to attract foreign entrepreneurs. I think that any increased effort should await the evaluation of the existing pilot scheme running approx. A year and a half more. Against this background, the government considers that there is no basis for further work on the establishment of entrepreneurship houses, work and travel grants and more residence permits for foreign entrepreneurs. For the same reason, the government cannot support the two motions for resolutions tabled. However, I would also like to signal very clearly here, when dealing with the two motions for resolutions, that the whole question of entrepreneurship is crucial for Denmark, which is why I also acknowledge the fact that the debate has been raised. As I mentioned earlier, and as I have clarified in various contexts, I also believe that further entrepreneurship initiatives need to be taken. It is my intention that in the autumn, the government will present new and targeted proposals that will help to strengthen our entrepreneurial culture in Denmark, not least in order to ensure that the entrepreneurs who start up will be able to scale up their business. There, in relation to the many global opportunities that exist in a digital world, I think it is a very, very important issue to put on the political agenda. With these comments, I would like to say that we cannot support the proposals, but that does not mean that the debate is not important. That's it.

For it is and remains the prerequisite for growth and prosperity that we have companies that are established, grow and create value and jobs and contribute to the prosperity of Denmark. Because it is the case that this type of service will mean that a company will be established and run with public crowns in its backs, while an existing company in the same field will not have the same public support in its backs. Thus, an otherwise well-run company may risk being out-dated by a company with public support in its backs, and not much growth and employment is created by simply replacing existing companies with new ones. So it is not a model and a scheme that we can support from the Left. In relation to the second resolution on an international entrepreneurship programme and international greenhouses, it can be said that these are very good intentions in this area, in other words to ensure that Danish entrepreneurs look outwards and that it is also attractive for foreign entrepreneurs to establish themselves in Denmark. But we therefore believe that these are other things that are necessary to further strengthen entrepreneurship in Denmark.

ParlaMint-DK_20160513141109	Thank you. As several have said, we are discussing the two motions for resolutions from the Alternative, which have the overall theme of increasing entrepreneurship in Denmark. They have to be collected from some companies, even from some other entrepreneurs, which reduces employment. So it's not something that will increase entrepreneurship in Denmark other than that it will distort competition in relation to the entrepreneurs who won't be on this service. There is no entrepreneurship in it. Then there is the idea of creating an international entrepreneurship programme with two entrepreneurship houses. We already have similar measures. There are greenhouses, etc. etc. One can discuss how much entrepreneurship it creates, but at least it already exists. Then there is an idea that you should offer 100 travel grants to Danish entrepreneurs. I think scholarship is a fine thing, but it is not something that the public should finance. It is fine when private people take such initiatives when civil society enters and helps young people or older people, or who would like to be an entrepreneur, with some grants, but that is not something other taxpayers should finance. Then there's the idea that I might think is the weirdest of these ideas, and that's that you want to give 100 work and residence permits to international entrepreneurs, and they will then receive an initial capital of DKK 250,000 when they come to Denmark. In addition to the fact that I find it strange that there should be some people sitting there and assessing who are future foreign entrepreneurs, I have to say that I think it is wildly provocative. In other words, the idea is almost provocative that taxpayers, including entrepreneurs, should help to finance the arrival of foreign entrepreneurs in Denmark, and that they got seed capital when you think about how difficult it is for Danish entrepreneurs to get seed capital. Well, I think it's an absurd idea. Foreign entrepreneurs should be more than welcome in Denmark, more than welcome. We have said in our entrepreneurial programme that they must be very welcome, as I have just said, but when they come here they must have their own initial capital. This is not something we have to finance for them. Having said that, I would, of course, like to congratulate the alternative for putting the debate on the agenda. Entrepreneurs are needed in Denmark, for self-employed people in Denmark to jump into starting their own business. Several have mentioned that Denmark is a good place to operate; it is often mentioned in the debate. On the list of easy of doing business, which is often mentioned, we are in 2016 in third place. But it is the case to do business in Denmark in general. If you make the effort to delve into the report, you can see that when it comes to starting your own business, we are in a 29th place in Denmark. So Denmark is not a particularly attractive place to start your business. And what is it that pulls down in this report when they look at Denmark? There are a number of things that do, but it is very much taxation in Denmark. We have created an entrepreneurial initiative that addresses the challenges of starting a business in Denmark. We have a large number of proposals • we have so many that I can hardly reach them all here. But what goes through with our proposal is that we have actually been based on what research knows is important for entrepreneurship. And it is not all sorts of small schemes that may sound interesting. Those are the overall framework conditions. We have, for example. proposed what we call a 10-10 deduction. All Danes have a deduction if they start business, and if you start a business and work in it yourself and you sell it at some point, you have a deduction of 10 million. kr., and you have to pay 10%. in tax, if you sell your business. We have a proposal that there should be better opportunities to make deficits. Many start-ups have deficits in the first few years, and there is a limited possibility of making these losses, which have been accumulated in previous years. And this is an obstacle to many entrepreneurs. We had a proposal to make the framework better for employee shares. It may be difficult for new companies to attract employees because they find it difficult to pay a high salary, and thus it is a good idea that the company can then offer them some employee shares on some advantageous terms. This was a concrete proposal that we included in the financial law negotiations and which we actually managed to implement. Then we have a complete abolition of the entrepreneurial tax, which, contrary to what has been said earlier on in the Parliament's platform, has not been completely abolished. It is still the case that a company which is a shareholder of another company, i.e. an unlisted company and which has less than 10%. the share holding is taxed more heavily than if it had a share holding of more than 10%. in the company where there is no taxation. Then we have some of the most important things, and this is the capital thing. And it has to be said that capital taxation in Denmark is probably the biggest obstacle to entrepreneurship. That is why we believe both that the share tax should be reduced, but also that corporate tax should be reduced. This means a greater profit for the companies and the profits are being invested in other companies. Then we have our proposal of no tax on the first 7,000 kroner. The Left is very concerned about this concept of poverty. However, when you dive into the analyses made by the Ministry of Business and Growth and look at what kind of group is described as poor, it turns out that the third largest single group in the group of poor is actually self-employed. And I think that's almost provocative, so that we have a tax system in Denmark that makes many people have higher income on transfer than when you start your own business. So, of course, we need a tax in Denmark that does not pay taxes until you can support yourself. Those were some of our suggestions. We also have some debureaucratisation proposals, for example. one entrance to the SKAT, which will mean that the bureaucracy and the large number of telephone calls that you sometimes have to make when you have to contact an authority when you start a new business will be reduced. So we cannot support this proposal. Although we think it is good that entrepreneurship is put on the agenda, this is not a proposal that will make Denmark an entrepreneurial nation. Thank you.

And if they are suddenly about to be on transfer income and state-aided instead of being seen as what they are, namely some who invest their lives, and at the beginning of their project everything they own and have, because they believe so much in it, then I do not really think it benefits entrepreneurship. I do not think it is for the benefit of people who are on a transfer income or on a daily allowance to believe that being an entrepreneur is something you do equally. It is extremely difficult and requires your whole life... blood, sweat and tears... and we therefore believe that if it is an employment project, it must be in the Ministry of Employment, and if it is entrepreneurship, you must support them in the ways I outlined in my speech.

ParlaMint-DK_20160513143451	We are working together on the two proposals from the Alternative for an international entrepreneurship programme with two international entrepreneurship houses and on a 2-year targeted entrepreneurial service. And I have some mixed comments on them. I think it is very good that we are discussing entrepreneurship. Before I was elected to the Danish Parliament, I worked at Aalborg University's entrepreneurial office, where we actually tried to get the students started, perhaps even before they were finished; with some of the things they did PhD in or wrote paper about. So it's really, really exciting. It also gave me a little insight into how many offers there are actually out there. I do not think that this is the way it is perceived by those who want to start. For them, I think more it seems like a jungle, or as if there's exactly what they need most. So I would like us to take a look at these proposals and see if we could sort out some of these many offers. There are many offers for entrepreneurs. There's VOICE. There's the Entrepreneurship Aid. Just in Aalborg, which I know best, there is BusinessAalborg and entrepreneurship education. And there is entrepreneurial help in the greenhouses. So maybe I think it's more about looking at how we can undo this. This does not mean that there should be less help or less economic opportunities for help, but that there should be a little more transparency in it. Because there is some overlap really, very many places. However, I think that the proposal for an entrepreneurial benefit on a reduced daily allowance rate is a very good idea. The SF would like to support that. We are not sure that this should be the way it should be put together, but we will support the proposal. We think the intention is really good. It is relatively easy to start a business in Denmark. What is difficult is to make it survive for just a year, for example. We see a lot of bucks under there. But many also become wiser by having tried and then they get up and try again. And that may be what we need to support. Well, there's the culture at least in the United States, where the Tax Committee was on a trip to Silicon Valley, so that you can actually get better and better by trying, so you can fail, but you can get up again. That is also what we need to help with. It may not succeed the first time, but then you can try again. And there'll be a reduced daily allowance bet there'll be a safety net. Of course, this does not help what many people find difficult, namely access to capital and so on. But I also think that we should be careful not to suck the state too much into that part of it. There's business angels out there, there's incubator programs, and there's capital, etc. And there must also be a certain degree of market element in it, i.e. in whether the ideas are so good that they can attract capital. But it's about getting started at all. It was the same with my own brother-in-law who wanted to get started on a daily allowance rate. And even if you are actually an active jobseeker and are looking for a job while trying to start something up in your spare time, then if it is to be settled again, you can suddenly sit in an economic, systemic catache. So I also believe that there is even more to be done here by saying that you should not be penalised financially because you have tried and actually also complied with your obligations in relation to being job seekers within the existing system or even if it was converted into an entrepreneurial benefit. SF has some funding proposals. I think this could be self-financing, as they say, in the short term, but that is not how they count in the Ministry of Finance. So we also have some ideas for immediate funding, for example. by really tightening up the research tax scheme. In the SF we would like to have the discussion about whether it should go ahead at all, but if it should, it should be tightened up. It is used in the East and the West and often for some rather high-paid jobs in our medico companies and elsewhere, and we do not think that is fair. So there we want to tighten up and spend some of the funding to strengthen what we must also live on in the future, namely to get young people in particular to come in and help solve some of the challenges and problems facing society. That is also what I am reading into the alternative's proposal: especially with regard to the social, sustainable, environmental and climate challenges that you are facing, there are things that we would like to have all the good powers and minds in play to solve. And it is fantastic if there are people who can make money solving concrete social challenges. What is there of public support crowns and offers? In fact, some of them are opposed to each other because it will be more about a competition for what is just being done with support measures than that there should be an overall offer to entrepreneurs across the country. I could have doubts as to whether this is the best way of doing things today. In relation to the entrepreneurial houses, I would say that there are a lot of entrepreneurial houses today, but they have grown from the bottom up. And I actually think that's an even more creative way for people to get together. So that part of the proposal we in the SF are not so crazy about. We are more concerned with securing both the framework for the entrepreneurial environments that are there, but also the advice that needs to be provided. So we are not so interested in making state-funded entrepreneurial houses, but more in looking at how the environments that crawl up and start on their own and that jump out can be associated with the advice that we would like to help them with. That's it. So from here there is support for the proposal for a 2-year targeted entrepreneurial service and partial support for some of the things that lie in the second proposal. However, we would rather spend the money on supporting some of the environments that already exist, rather than setting up new concrete entrepreneurship houses.

ParlaMint-DK_20160513144322	Thank you. I would like to start by thanking the alternative for the initiative. This is close to the most important issue we can discuss here in the Parliament: how do we get some more entrepreneurs? How do we get more people to take responsibility for their own lives, and how do we create more activity in Danish society? This is necessary because growth is at snail speed in Denmark. We can see that there is a lack of surviving entrepreneurs. Denmark is an easy country to start business in. It's relatively unbureaucratic. Starting a business is relatively cheap. But we can see that the rate of survival is not very high in Denmark in terms of creating jobs and growth. That is why the alternative's approach is very good, and we would very much like to discuss it more. As far as the concrete proposals are concerned, we have been through entrepreneurship before. We were in the 1990s, and I myself was then employed as an economist in the unemployment fund for self-employed people, ASE, as it is called. There we also had a lot on entrepreneurship. What was the conclusion of a number of studies carried out at that time, not by ASE, but by SFI and others, was that many of the companies on entrepreneurship did not survive. They kind of pushed the effect. Firstly, there was a distortion of competition in the creation of entrepreneurial services and the creation of a business right next to a business that did not receive entrepreneurial services. Secondly, it was like a pretext that you might just as well do it, instead of having it in your blood, in your DNA, that you would start your own business with blood, sweat and tears. Therefore, the survival rate of the companies that were set up because of entrepreneurship was very low! This does not mean that you should not look without prejudice at how to get people on transfer income to start their own business. We would like to look at this because it would be win-win for Danish society to move people away from passive support on one or other service and to go out and start their own business and hopefully also build it up so much that they can live on it and maybe also get some employees and can develop. But we don't believe in that part of it. Then there is B 133 around international entrepreneurship houses and fellows etc. Since there are now all the arrangements in Danish society in that area, I do not think that this is where we should start. I think that what is important here is to look at how we create some conditions so that people want to start their own business. It goes all the way back to school. One has to look at how to create a situation in school so that people are not just trained to be public employees or employees, but want to start their own business. If you went into a school class today, such as one of my daughters' school classes, and asked them if they wanted to start their own business, then not many would answer yes. I myself went to school in the United States, and there you could do a similar show of hands. There would certainly have been more than two thirds of my classmates saying: I would like to start my own business while in Denmark you experience exactly the opposite. So it's all about mentality right back in school: that you want to take on the responsibility of wanting to set up a company and want to develop it yourself and have the dream to do so. So the first thing we need to do is to do something about the school so that we also get trained people who take responsibility themselves and entrepreneurs at school. The second is that we need to look at where the greatest challenge for entrepreneurs is today. The greatest challenge for entrepreneurs today, these two proposals do not address, but the alternative has been mentioned at other times about the financing of entrepreneurs, which I think has been very sensible. There is a lack of liquidity as an entrepreneur. One lacks an opportunity to invest. One lacks the opportunity to innovate as an entrepreneur. So the greatest challenge you have as an entrepreneur today is the capital that you need. There we in the Conservative People's Party have specifically proposed the creation of a share-saving account which corresponds to the model in Sweden. We proposed during last year's financial law negotiations, and we will repeat it again this autumn, that as a small saver we simply have an opportunity to invest in start-ups, small businesses, etc., so that they get the necessary boost in terms of capital, which can help boost them and secure the foundations under them. So these are two of the key areas where action must be taken, and there is, of course, a whole range of other initiatives that you can look at: making life less difficult, providing better framework conditions for entrepreneurs and also better counselling at several different levels. So, as much as I hate to say it, the Conservative People's Party cannot vote in favour of these two proposals. However, we would like to congratulate the alternative for raising the issue and also for constantly launching the discussion and also for doing so with some of the other proposals that have been put forward here. However, these two proposals cannot be supported by the Conservative People's Party.

ParlaMint-DK_20160513144906	Thank you. Today we are discussing, in addition to B 134, which must be dealt with afterwards, two proposals which the Alternative has put forward with the aim of strengthening entrepreneurship in Denmark. The two proposals concern the introduction of an entrepreneurial service and the creation of an international entrepreneurship programme. The proposals are part of our entrepreneurial package, a entrepreneurial Denmark, which aims to raise entrepreneurship to new heights, because it is our ambition that the 21st century should be the century of entrepreneurs, and here we actually believe that the two proposals under discussion today can play a really important role. I am, of course, pleased with the nice words that have come from all the rapporteurs, and also with the long list of very good ideas that several people have listed and which, of course, point out that we here in the Danish Parliament want entrepreneurship, and I look forward to seeing that, in the course of the discussion of these proposals, we will hopefully also get even more proposals on the table so that we can do something together by raising entrepreneurship very high. For the alternative, it is a matter of priority that Denmark should have a stronger and even more socially beneficial entrepreneurial culture. The small and medium-sized enterprises are, after all, some of those who employ the most people in Denmark, so we would like to have more of them, and we pay tribute to the progressive pioneers and the value-based communities who have the imagination and the courage to challenge the existing one in order to bring about a positive change. But it has to be easier to do, easier to make a difference. For the future Denmark must be a much more entrepreneurial, entrepreneurial and creative society than it is today, and therefore we will work to change some of the rigid structures that prevent many emerging entrepreneurial talents from flourishing, so that more can go from being job-taking creators It is only how we believe that Denmark can become the best country, not just in the world, but the best country for the world. It is only the case that we can find solutions to the acute challenges we face, for example. more traditional jobs disappear, and the climate crisis calls for completely new answers because it forces us to change the way we produce, transport and organise ourselves. In relation to the concrete proposals, we want to introduce, with the first proposal, an entrepreneurial benefit for the unemployed on unemployment benefits, which has a budding entrepreneur in the stomach. Instead of subjecting benefit recipients to restrictive controls and rigid rules, we will meet more with confidence and with a framework that allows us to pursue dreams of being self-employed. In Denmark, we have a labour market and a social safety net that will easily support an even more dynamic entrepreneurial culture. But unfortunately, instead, it often seems that we are afraid to unleash our entrepreneurial talents. For example: Today many unemployed people are prevented from jumping out as entrepreneurs because of rigid rules that they have to be available for the labour market in a very specific way. However, this does not change the fact that the public has the potential to help people from public support to a life as self-employed if we know how to use our resources much better. And that's why we're suggesting that the unemployed should be able to come up with this 2-year entrepreneurial benefit. There has been a lot of criticism today that we have already tried it and that it did not work, but we should not look at this again. But what is important to do when you look at experiences is also to look at experiences from other parts of the world, and especially as regards the way in which this is thought of together with the support that also lies in it in relation to mentoring, etc., it is of course experiences from Sweden, from Norway and from Germany that have shown that it actually works and it is sensible. So instead of looking only at bad experiences from Denmark, we have actually chosen to look at good experiences from other countries. The Enhedslisten was talking about whether there is a lack of skills in the A-boxes to help people. It may well be that it does too, but I believe that there is a very great lack of rules that will allow people to do so. As the SF rapporteur said, we are putting up barriers for people who want to, and that is also why I find it interesting that the Mayor of Copenhagen has been out with a number of cashiers and saying that it would be sensible to look at what we can do here and how we can use our unemployment benefit system to create jobs for the future. So yes, I think we should also look at the 'A' boxes and whether there might be something there, but I think the important thing in this proposal is that we take it seriously as entrepreneurship. It is not employment, it is entrepreneurship, and I think it is important that it should not just be in the a-boxes, but that it should be in the business area. In our second proposal on the creation of the international entrepreneurial programme with entrepreneurship houses, we want to take a step towards making Denmark the best country to be an entrepreneur in, with good external experience, mixed with good talents in Denmark. Firstly, the proposal is about attracting international entrepreneurs from all over the world, and it is inspired by programmes from among others. Chile. Here you have good experience, which shows that by providing particularly favourable conditions to international entrepreneurs, you can actually strengthen your own entrepreneurial culture and your own entrepreneurial businesses. But by offering these jobs, which bring the international entrepreneurs together with the Danish entrepreneurs, we will not just create another entrepreneurship house. Because we are fully aware that there are good and amazing entrepreneurship houses and office communities and everything else that works very well, and as many have also mentioned, they are created from below and they can do a lot of things. But what we're going to achieve by creating some specific houses that bring together entrepreneurs in these groups is to create some networks. Because something happens when you sit with other entrepreneurs and work on ideas. That is precisely why many of the houses we already have are well-functioning. Because these are the networks you create, and here we will focus on the international part of it. So, yes, we really want this entrepreneurship, and I am very pleased with the debate that has taken place today, where all the rapporteurs have come up with good ideas, and that is why we are also looking forward to the work of the committee on this, and we still hope that we can push something forward. Not least in relation to the Minister's words about the government's work on entrepreneurship, we hope that we can also help to inspire with these proposals and to push a little on that agenda with the government as well. Thank you, however, to all the rapporteurs.

So this is something that concerns people and politicians in countries all over the world because it's all about the social, economic and sustainable bottom line, and because it's really, really expensive for a society to only be interested in the economic bottom line and just say to a lot of people: we can't use you, mind your own business. It is not worthy of human dignity, and it is also murky in economic terms. ParlaMint-DK_20160513154538	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for Industry and Growth.
ParlaMint-DK_20160513154610	Thank you. The authors of the motion for a resolution want to regulate the possibility for mortgage credit institutions to increase the rates of contributions and price cut fees. In concrete terms, it is proposed that the rates of contributions and price cut fees should not be increased during the duration of the loan. In specific cases where stricter regulatory requirements involve increased capital requirements, it is proposed that supervisory authorities may grant derogations to temporarily increase contribution rates. In these cases, the FSA must ensure that the contribution rates correspond only to the increased capital requirement and that the rates are reduced once the capital requirement is met. If the rule does not increase the contribution rates, the mortgage credit institution becomes, among other things, the mortgage credit institution. are required not to pay dividends to the owner or bonus to the management for a period of 2 years. I would like to start by stating that it is very important for the government to ensure good lending opportunities for Danish home owners in all parts of Denmark. I also believe that our special Danish mortgage system contributes to this. At the same time, however, I would like to make it quite clear that the price controls proposed in the motion for a resolution are not a sensible way to go. It is a road that risks making Danish mortgage loans more expensive. The primary source of revenue for mortgage credit institutions is the contribution rates, and thus the proposed limitation will be a major influence on the profit base of institutions. This can have both serious and inappropriate consequences for institutions and also for borrowers. The need to increase revenue is prompted not only by increased capital requirements but also by a wide range of other factors. The motion for a resolution overlooks the fact that mortgage banks may experience unforeseen losses on loans resulting from property price falls and financial crises. In addition, increased profitability requirements may also arise from rating agencies, investors, etc. If price controls are introduced into the mortgage market, the Danish mortgage institutions will not be able to trade and meet market requirements and expectations. This is potentially going to damage the financial markets' confidence in mortgage banks. A deterioration in confidence can include: result in a impaired rating on the bonds. This means, of course, that the price of the bonds will fall and the interest rate will rise, and as the interest rate on mortgage bonds is the rate paid by the borrower, the proposal will therefore entail higher interest rates for all home owners. Overall, the proposal can therefore make it more expensive to obtain mortgage loans for the Danish homeowners. Moreover, the proposed limitations on the earnings potential of institutions may result in the contribution rate being higher at the time of the conclusion of the loan agreement than at the present time. This can be done to ensure the institution's coverage of increased costs which may arise during the life of the loan. It is the government's view that it is free competition in the credit market that creates the best opportunities for customers to obtain the good and cheap loans. That is the competition that we must ensure and strengthen, and this is not done by introducing price controls. In order to increase transparency and consumer protection in the setting of interest and charges by financial undertakings, I tightened the rules in the autumn, among other things. in relation to the change of interest and fees in current customer relations and the warning of such changes. That was, for example, the case. specified that financial undertakings must provide adequate justification for changes in fees and interest rates. The rules also apply to mortgage credit institutions. The government wants to create further clarity in this area. Against this background, I have launched a number of initiatives. First of all, as has already been mentioned, I have set up a committee of experts in this Parliament to look at the transparency and mobility of the mortgage market. The Committee of Experts shall, inter alia: the development of the contribution rates is addressed, including how future capital requirements affect mortgage credit institutions as well as the costs associated with the customer's change of mortgage credit institution. The report of the Committee shall be ready by 1 September and shall include recommendations for necessary adjustments to the rules. Finally, I can also inform you that the Competition Council will examine the competition in the mortgage market and then a price portal for mortgage loans will be launched before the end of the year so that Danish home owners can compare prices and find the cheapest loans. It's even part of a broad political agreement that translates this price portal. In view of the above reasons, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution tabled.

ParlaMint-DK_20160513165724	Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mrs Josephine Fock, the alternative.
ParlaMint-DK_20160513165740	Thank you for the floor. The Danish mortgage system is the best in the world. It has historically been the cheapest, and it has also historically been the safest. This has been the case, and we want it to stay that way. When you take a loan, it is important that you know on what terms you borrow. It is important that you know how much you have to pay in advance every month, so you know how much you have after loans and rents. But you can't be sure about that today. Even customers with fixed-rate loans, where payments are immediately believed to be firm, may run the risk of their contributions rising. And, as the rules are today, there is no ceiling on how much mortgage credit institutions can raise the contribution rates. That is why we are tabling this motion for a resolution. The Danish cooperative funds came out of some of the things we are best at in Denmark, namely to cooperate through cooperative movements. The mortgage started in the late 1700s after the big fire at Holmen in Copenhagen in 1795 where 900 houses burned down. After the fire, the many new homeless needed new loans for new houses, so they joined forces and started the first mortgage. Historically, therefore, mortgage institutions are in many ways a pillar of the cooperative movement, which in the last 200 years has provided loans for high and low. A mortgage consists of three parts: interest, instalments and contributions. Interest and instalments will be determined by the customer and the mortgage institution working together to determine which loan the customer is to receive and how long to repay. Then the mortgage credit institution takes it to the bond market, where it is sold to the one who is willing to give the lowest interest rates. The mortgage institutions must convey and administer the loan, and that, of course, costs some money. That is what the rates of contributions should in principle be. Historically, therefore, the mortgage banks are just a sort of intermediary who has to provide loans between the borrower and the lender. Because mortgage banks have been co-funded, i.e. owned by the borrowers, the only interest for mortgage banks has been to keep the contribution rates as low as possible. Therefore, it has not been necessary to make legislation which regulated the contribution rates. But in 1989 a reform of the mortgage was carried out. It meant that mortgage institutions no longer had to be cooperative. Now banks and external investors could also set up mortgage institutions. This has meant that over the last 25 years we have seen a trend towards more and more commercial mortgage institutions, and today Nykredit is the last of the large mortgage institutions that are client-owned, and Nykredit bars already with a stock exchange quote. The disappearance of the cooperative mortgage credit institution is not only a pity for the democratic cooperative movement, which counts after heads and not after head, but also a problem for borrowers. In fact, this means that the mortgage banks go from only having to look after the interests of the borrowers to now also looking after the interests of the external shareholders. And since mortgage banks only provide loans, the only place they can make money for their shareholders is through the fees and contributions rates. Shareholders are therefore interested in as high contribution rates as possible, but borrowers are interested in as low contribution rates as possible. This has resulted in a conflict of interests, and that is why we are proposing to change the law. Sometimes it may be necessary for a mortgage institution to raise the contribution rates. It's always been like that. At the moment, however, mortgage credit institutions face some additional challenges because, following the financial crisis, they are being imposed on more and more capital requirements. Therefore, it must also be possible for mortgage credit institutions to increase the contribution rates, but only if it is approved by an independent body such as the European Parliament. FSA or the Competition Authority. In this way, we can ensure that contributions are raised only if there is a real need and not just to pay extra dividends to shareholders. Therefore, it is also natural that conditions should apply if mortgage credit institutions raise their contribution rates. Firstly, it must not be possible for mortgage banks to pay dividends to shareholders two years after they have raised the contribution rates. We think this is quite natural. Because if it is strictly necessary to increase the contribution rates, there should also be no money to pay dividends to shareholders. Secondly, if one's contribution rate is raised, it must be possible to change mortgage institutions at the least cost possible. In many other situations in society, it is not a big problem if prices are raised. If, for example, the price... is put up on oatmeal in the yellow package I usually buy, yes, so I just choose oatmeal in the red or green package. But you can't do that with mortgages. If you have to change mortgages today, there are huge costs, both because, for example, there are. may be price losses on the bond to which the loan is tied and because the mortgage institution to which you move charges a conversion fee. That is why many borrowers are now in a mess here, where the rates of contributions are being raised. It's both expensive to stay, and it's expensive to change. That is why we are also proposing that if a mortgage credit institution raises the contribution rates on a loan, it should not impose charges beyond the administrative costs that the mortgage credit institution can demonstrate, for example in relation to. loan restructuring. As I said, we have the best mortgage system in the world, and it must remain so. It's the place in the world where you can get the cheapest loans.
