ParlaMint-DK_20160513172803	A few months ago, hundreds of thousands of Danes woke up to a really boring surprise, namely that one of Denmark's largest mortgage institutions simply set up the fees for their mortgage loans. For some, it was many thousands of kroner, that is, families who have put themselves in a loan, have expected and have even been informed that this was the safe type of loan, but that suddenly gets knocked out of their household budget. In a month and a half, on 1 July, they'll raise the fees. You just got a few months to prepare for it, and then we'll see if we can afford the summer holidays in many of those families. With good reason, it has triggered very massive protests from many of the customers. The Facebook Group, called "Critical stakeholders" in Nykredit and Totalkredit, is followed by more than 40,000 Danes who are debating. It is Danes who are committed. An association has been formed with many thousands of members, called "fair rate of contribution'. I would actually like to take this opportunity to thank the many thousands of Danes who have interfered in this debate, put pressure on Nykredit, put pressure on politicians and send good ideas and views to us. I think many others have received that too. The motion for a resolution we have tabled together with the SF and the alternative is very much the result of the good ideas which have come from many of the citizens' initiatives in this field and the economists with whom they have allied. After all, the proposal's intention is basically to regulate the possibility for mortgage credit institutions to increase their contribution rates. Why is this so necessary? Well, it's because of one, I think, sad development in our mortgage system. As the rapporteur for the Alternative also mentioned, our mortgage system has been unique: customer-owned and customer-controlled mortgage associations. It was citizens who joined forces to create mortgage associations to ensure cheap housing funding. It was the customers who owned the mortgage and they functioned very much as such some resting in themselves. No one outside had to make money to offer mortgage loans to the Danes and the rates of contributions, and that is what this is all about, i.e. the rates of contributions or fees, had to cover only the administrative costs and possible losses for which it was then collectively liable. As the years have passed, the Danish mortgage credit system has unfortunately increasingly been integrated into the financial supermarkets. A number of mortgage banks have been bought up by banks, and the reason for Nykredit's new fee explosion is precisely that the management wants to stock exchange the company. However, with the transformation of mortgage associations from democratic, customer-owned companies to financial supermarkets owned by outside shareholders, a problem has arisen. For where no one had an interest in raising the contribution rates when the association was a customer-owned, the contribution rates have now become a possible tool to increase earnings to shareholders and the banks that own the mortgage. Contributions and fees have become a money machine that can be turned up at will. That is why we need to regulate how much and when mortgage credit institutions have to increase their contribution rates. That's what this proposal is about. It is simply that over the last 20 years our mortgage system has changed fundamentally, but we have the old regulatory regime and it does not catch the problems that arise here. So our proposal will set clear limits on when mortgage credit institutions have the opportunity to change the terms of loan contracts already concluded. As a starting point, customers should be able to rely on being able to plan their private economy in an appropriate way by knowing that these rates of contribution cannot be increased on loans already made. Then we acknowledge in the Enhedslisten and the Alternative and SF that there may be situations, for example. In these cases, mortgage credit institutions must of course be given the opportunity to do so, but it should not be a self-delivered roof. They must be able to prove that this is the reason for it. They must also commit themselves to reducing the contribution rates once they have obtained that capitalisation and, above all, they must not be able to use that capitalisation to increase their dividend payments once they have just increased the contribution rates. So these are the demands we make in order to be allowed to raise the rates of contributions. Then there are several of the rapporteurs who have said in this debate that the most important thing is to create competition and that in itself it will push down the contributions. I also think it is important to create competition. The problem is that there will probably never be a complete free competition in this area, because the cost of changing the loan provider is so high and they will be, because it is not just the fees, it is also the fact that you may have taken up a loan at a time when the interest rate was low, but when the interest rate then rises, you are stuck in your loan. It is just as in the nature of these long-term loans that there will never be a proper functioning of competition, and that also applies to other areas of our society, and there we have also taken responsibility as a society and said that in those cases, for example. monopolies and other things, there has to be regulation. So we want to help to increase competition, and we are also proposing a number of measures in this proposal, which may not have been noticed so much, among other things. That is to say, when contributions are raised, we should be right to move cheaper to another mortgage institution, for example. by not using these price cut fees. So the idea of this proposal from Enhedslisten, Alternativet og SF is really just to ensure a greater degree of fairness in the way in which the contribution rates are administered; the customers simply need to have greater security and we will defend that the Danish mortgage must continue to be one of the cheapest things that this development unfortunately threatens. We have offered both the Social Democrats and the Danish People's Party to be co-sponsors of the proposal, because we took note that when this happened, there were, we think, positive notes from both parties that this was something that should be taken seriously, but there was no interest in being co-sponsors. However, I would like to acknowledge that there have been a number of speeches from up here in which it is recognised that there is a problem. I think a lot of the people who watch out there are happy about that. But there are just up to half a million Danes who will be affected by these fees in a month and a half, and when we have made this proposal, it is of course to try to help them. I look forward, of course, to a report from the government in the autumn so that we can see if it can end up with something. It just doesn't help the people who've gotten stuck here and now. However, I would like to thank you for an interesting and good debate. I think that this issue is really one of the really, really important issues, because the housing economy is probably one of the most important for real, very many people, and that is why I think it is good that it has been taken seriously. We may disagree with what needs to be done, but I am glad that there is the attention on this issue.

Equality at the top is not a solution in itself, but can help to highlight other and more serious inequalities for women, for example. women generally earn 17%. less than the men. I think that it provides an opportunity for there to be a follow-up and that there may be a reflection in management, management and board, and focusing on the value of having versatile composite boards can thus be the opening to a greater variation in management, which I have often experienced is an advantage. It is a disadvantage if you have boards which consist exclusively of seven male lawyers aged 50+ or seven male machine masters of the same age. It is an advantage to have versatility on the boards. I can hear that there are some women in this Chamber who think that it is a disadvantage if you just have a little doubt that you have been appointed to sit on a board or be in a management because you are a woman. I have an experience of being on board with very talented women, and I also have experience in helping to persuade women to run for office. It sometimes takes a little longer, because those women would very much like to fully fulfil the task they undertake and are probably a little more critical of themselves, as they think they should have all the competences from the start of a task rather than having the approach to what many men have, namely that it is going well. So I feel that this doubt does not have to be so much, because there are so many well-qualified women in society, and women make up half of society, so it is no problem to have targets that help to focus on the fact that there must also be women in management. This may help to open up the possibility of looking at what, by the way, is a versatility which one can benefit from. Is it a plus on a board to have someone who's not exactly like the others? Is it a plus maybe having a less than 30 years, if that's how the rest of the board is 60+? I think you should look very carefully at what is the best composition on a board, and I think these targets about gender can be a tool for management to be pressured to reflect on it and try to look at what it is for any skills that are needed. You can have some good processes where you do board evaluations and try to find out if there are any areas of competence that are not enough on a board, and what it is you have to go after getting appointed. So for those women who are concerned about sitting on a board because they reflect on whether they sit there because they are women, I would say that I think there are many lawyers sitting around on boards who do not at all reflect on whether they sit on that board, because all the others on the same board have the same age and the same professional background. So I think it's time to be careful. The fact that you have these targets and work with it is positive to me.

ParlaMint-DK_20160513193817	First of all, thank you for a very good debate. The primary focus of companies is on the bottom line, and therefore their only interest is to hire the people both on the floor and in the managements that the company believes they are absolutely best qualified to contribute to growth and a positive bottom line. A company does not separate itself from gender and race and sexuality, because the only thing important to the company is qualifications, because it is the qualified candidate who contributes to the best bottom line. Therefore, it is also the company which is the best at putting together the optimal management. With this motion for a resolution, the Liberal Alliance will give companies the freedom to be able to focus solely on qualifications and not have to take into account everything else, because we believe that the only responsibility a company actually has is to make profits for its shareholders, and we believe that the company must have the freedom to live up to that. After all, it also benefits society as a whole when companies grow. The demand for targets and the development of a policy to promote the underrepresented sex is unnecessary bureaucracy to impose on Danish companies. It is an economic burden, and the competitiveness of Danish companies is already under massive pressure, so the last thing they need is for us in this Parliament to impose additional burdens on them. Eliminating this law will thus improve the framework conditions for Danish companies, and Denmark needs this. More parties consider it problematic that there are more men represented in management than women. In the Liberal Alliance, we do not think it is problematic that people have different priorities.
